=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array of strings words and an integer k.
For each index i in the range [0, words.length - 1], find the length of the longest common prefix among any k strings (selected at distinct indices) from the remaining array after removing the i^th element.
Return an array answer, where answer[i] is the answer for i^th element. If removing the i^th element leaves the array with fewer than k strings, answer[i] is 0.
 
Example 1:

Input: words = ["jump","run","run","jump","run"], k = 2
Output: [3,4,4,3,4]
Explanation:

Removing index 0 ("jump"):

	
words becomes: ["run", "run", "jump", "run"]. "run" occurs 3 times. Choosing any two gives the longest common prefix "run" (length 3).


Removing index 1 ("run"):
	
words becomes: ["jump", "run", "jump", "run"]. "jump" occurs twice. Choosing these two gives the longest common prefix "jump" (length 4).


Removing index 2 ("run"):
	
words becomes: ["jump", "run", "jump", "run"]. "jump" occurs twice. Choosing these two gives the longest common prefix "jump" (length 4).


Removing index 3 ("jump"):
	
words becomes: ["jump", "run", "run", "run"]. "run" occurs 3 times. Choosing any two gives the longest common prefix "run" (length 3).


Removing index 4 ("run"):
	
words becomes: ["jump", "run", "run", "jump"]. "jump" occurs twice. Choosing these two gives the longest common prefix "jump" (length 4).




Example 2:

Input: words = ["dog","racer","car"], k = 2
Output: [0,0,0]
Explanation:

Removing any index results in an answer of 0.


 
Constraints:

1 <= k <= words.length <= 10^5
1 <= words[i].length <= 10^4
words[i] consists of lowercase English letters.
The sum of words[i].length is smaller than or equal 10^5.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def longestCommonPrefix(self, words: List[str], k: int) -> List[int]:
        
```

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Now, need implement assignment with intervals and DSU.

Let's define DFS order positions 0..n-1. We'll have arrays:
- `pos_to_word[pos]` = original index of word at this position.
- `tin[node]`, `tout[node]` in position coordinate.
- `word_pos[orig_idx]` maybe position of each word? Since duplicates at same node, multiple positions. For assignment, we only need positions, not original indices. But answer array indexed by original word index. When assigning a position pos, set ans[pos_to_word[pos]] = L. We can assign positions and then map. We need DSU over positions to skip assigned positions.

DSU "next unassigned" over positions:
- `parent = list(range(n+1))` where parent[i] points to next unassigned position >= i, with n sentinel.
- `find(i)` returns smallest unassigned position >= i.
- When assign position p, set parent[p] = find(p+1) (or parent[p] = p+1? Need union). Standard:
```
def find(x):
    while parent[x] != x:
        parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]
        x = parent[x]
    return x
```
Initialize parent[i]=i, parent[n]=n.
To assign all unassigned positions in range [l, r):
```
p = find(l)
while p < r:
    ans[pos_to_word[p]] = L
    parent[p] = find(p+1)
    p = find(p)  # now next
```
Total assignments n, each find near O(alpha). But note if we assign range [l,r) and there are already assigned positions inside, find skips them. Good.

Now, need think about "longest common prefix among any k strings" exact LCP length. We used prefix count condition. Need prove. For any subset S of k strings, let L = LCP length. Then all k strings share prefix of length L, so count of that prefix among remaining >=k. Conversely, if some prefix p of length L has count >=k among remaining, choose any k strings with prefix p; their LCP is at least L. Thus max exact LCP = max L with some prefix count >=k. Yes. Now, complexity: Building trie O(total length). DFS O(nodes + n). Processing depths O(max_len + total assignments * alpha). Need ensure assignment ranges total O(n) because each position assigned once. But assign_range may be called with ranges that include already assigned positions? We use DSU skip assigned, so each position assigned once. However, assign_range could be called for overlapping ranges over already assigned positions many times, but find skips them; if range contains no unassigned, find(l) returns maybe >=r quickly O(alpha). Number of assign_range calls? At each bad node up to 2, plus all-allowed. Number of bad nodes could be O(nodes) up to 1e5. Each call O(alpha) plus assignments. But if many assign_range calls on empty ranges, each find O(alpha), total O(nodes * alpha) ~ okay 1e5. But need be careful if assign_range called with l==r, we can skip to avoid find. Good.

Potential issue: DSU find with path compression and parent array size n+1. When assign p, set parent[p] = find(p+1). But if p+1 assigned too, find returns next unassigned. Good. Standard.

Need implement `assign_range(l, r, val)`:
```
def assign_range(l, r, val):
    if l >= r:
        return
    p = find(l)
    while p < r:
        ans[pos_to_word[p]] = val
        parent[p] = find(p + 1)
        p = find(p)  # or parent[p]? after setting parent[p] to next, p = find(p) returns next; could use p = parent[p]? Need find.
```
But if we set parent[p] = find(p+1), then `p = find(p)` will return parent[p] (since parent[p] points to next unassigned). Actually after `parent[p] = find(p+1)`, `find(p)` returns that next. So `p = parent[p]` maybe okay if parent[p] is root? find(p+1) returns root. But due to path compression, parent[p] is root. So p = parent[p]. But safer `p = find(p)`.
Need note `find(p+1)` when p+1 == n returns n. parent[n]=n. Good.

But there is a subtlety: If we assign a position p, and later call find on a previous position whose parent chain points to p, path compression will skip. Good.

Need traverse children and set tin/tout. A common iterative DFS:
```
stack = [(0, False)]
while stack:
    v, visited = stack.pop()
    if not visited:
        tin[v] = len(order)
        order.extend(ends[v])
        stack.append((v, True))
        # push children in any order
        for child in children[v].values():
            stack.append((child, False))
    else:
        tout[v] = len(order)
```

Now, let's think about interval assignment with DSU and current interval. Suppose current interval [cur_l, cur_r) and bad v interval [lv, rv). We use containment:
```
if lv <= cur_l and cur_r <= rv:
    # no assign
elif cur_l <= lv and rv <= cur_r:
    assign_range(cur_l, lv, d)
    assign_range(rv, cur_r, d)
    cur_l, cur_r = lv, rv
else:
    assign_range(cur_l, cur_r, d)
    cur_l = cur_r = 0
```
But what if intervals are disjoint but due to current interval empty? We skip if cur_l >= cur_r at top. Good.

Yes, because DFS preorder intervals of rooted tree are laminar. But wait our `order` includes terminal words at nodes before children. For any node, interval is contiguous. For two nodes, intervals are either disjoint or one contains the other. Yes.

Now, let's consider code details.

Pseudo:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestCommonPrefix(self, words: List[str], k: int) -> List[int]:
        n = len(words)
        ans = [0]*n
        if k > n:
            return ans
        # if n == k? We can still run; but maybe early return 0? Since after removing any, n-1 < k. Yes if n == k, answer all 0. We can early return to save. But algorithm also returns 0. Early return okay? Need if k=n, after removing leaves n-1<k, answer0. Yes. Could return ans immediately. But if we return early, no need build. This is a good optimization. However, if n==k, answer 0 regardless. Let's include early return for n == k? Wait if k=n, after removing any, remaining n-1 = k-1 < k, so answer0. Yes. But what if k=0? no. So if n == k: return [0]*n. But careful: If n=1,k=1, answer0. Good.
        # But if n == k, our algorithm would also work but early saves.
        max_len = max(len(w) for w in words)
        children = [{}]
        cnt = [0]
        depth = [0]
        ends = [[]]
        nodes_by_depth = [[] for _ in range(max_len+1)]
        for idx, w in enumerate(words):
            node = 0
            for d, ch in enumerate(w, 1):
                nxt = children[node].get(ch)
                if nxt is None:
                    nxt = len(children)
                    children[node][ch] = nxt
                    children.append({})
                    cnt.append(0)
                    depth.append(d)
                    ends.append([])
                    nodes_by_depth[d].append(nxt)
                node = nxt
                cnt[node] += 1
            ends[node].append(idx)
```
Need be careful with `dict.get(ch)` returning None if child index could be 0? Child indices start at 1, so None safe. But if using `if ch not in children[node]` maybe okay. Use `nxt = children[node].get(ch, -1)`.

Potential issue: If a word has length max_len, nodes_by_depth has index max_len. Good.

Build DFS:
```
num_nodes = len(children)
tin = [0]*num_nodes
tout = [0]*num_nodes
order = []
stack = [(0, False)]
while stack:
    v, visited = stack.pop()
    if not visited:
        tin[v] = len(order)
        order.extend(ends[v])
        stack.append((v, True))
        # push children
        for child in children[v].values():
            stack.append((child, False))
    else:
        tout[v] = len(order)
```
Need if root has no ends, tin[0]=0, tout[0]=n. Good.
But wait: In this iterative DFS, because we push marker then children, the marker is below children. However, we push all children; due to LIFO, the last child is processed first. That's fine. But the marker is below all children, so tout after all children. Good.
Need ensure `order` length n. Since each word appended exactly once at its terminal node. Good.

Compute good_count/bad_node:
```
good_count = [0]*(max_len+1)
bad_node = [-1]*(max_len+1)
for d in range(1, max_len+1):
    gc = 0
    bad = -1
    for v in nodes_by_depth[d]:
        if cnt[v] >= k:
            gc += 1
            if gc == 1:
                bad = v
    good_count[d] = gc
    if gc == 1 and cnt[bad] == k:
        bad_node[d] = bad
```
Need if gc>=2, bad_node remains -1. If gc==1 and cnt>k, bad_node -1. Good.

But wait: If gc==1 and cnt[bad] == k, bad_node set. If gc==1 and cnt[bad] > k, all allowed. If gc==0, bad_node -1.

DSU:
```
parent = list(range(n+1))
def find(x):
    while parent[x] != x:
        parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]
        x = parent[x]
    return x

def assign_range(l, r, val):
    if l >= r:
        return
    p = find(l)
    while p < r:
        ans[pos_to_word[p]] = val
        parent[p] = find(p+1)
        p = find(p)
```
Need `pos_to_word` array length n:
```
pos_to_word = [0]*n
for pos, idx in enumerate(order):
    pos_to_word[pos] = idx
```
Could also use order directly.

Process:
```
cur_l, cur_r = 0, n
for d in range(max_len, 0, -1):
    if cur_l >= cur_r:
        break
    bv = bad_node[d]
    if bv != -1:
        lv = tin[bv]; rv = tout[bv]
        if lv <= cur_l and cur_r <= rv:
            # current inside bad subtree; no assignment
            continue
        if cur_l <= lv and rv <= cur_r:
            # bad subtree inside current
            assign_range(cur_l, lv, d)
            assign_range(rv, cur_r, d)
            cur_l, cur_r = lv, rv
        else:
            # disjoint
            assign_range(cur_l, cur_r, d)
            cur_l = cur_r = 0
    else:
        if good_count[d] > 0:
            assign_range(cur_l, cur_r, d)
            cur_l = cur_r = 0
        # else no good, continue
return ans
```
Need if `good_count[d] > 0` and bad_node[d] == -1 means all allowed. Good.

But wait: What if `bad_node[d] != -1` and current interval is disjoint from v interval, assign all current. But is it possible current interval is disjoint from v interval but there are unassigned positions outside current interval that are inside v? No, current interval is all unassigned positions. If disjoint, all unassigned positions are outside v, so allowed. Assign them. Good.

Need if current interval is inside v, no assignment. But what if current interval is inside v and there are unassigned positions? They remain. Good.

Need if current interval contains v, assign outside v. But what about unassigned positions inside v that were already assigned? By invariant none. But assign_range outside v won't touch inside. Good.

Potential issue: If current interval contains v, but there are positions in current interval outside v that are already assigned? Invariant says no. But due to DSU, assign_range skips assigned anyway. Fine.

Need if current interval is all [0,n) and bad v interval [lv,rv). It contains v. assign [0,lv) and [rv,n). Good.

Now, let's test with a case where current interval is inside v but v interval is not ancestor? In laminar intervals, inside means ancestor. Good.

Now, let's think about if `good_count[d] > 0` and bad_node[d] == -1 but current interval maybe inside some bad node from higher depth? Doesn't matter; all allowed at depth d, assign remaining. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bad node at depth d but current interval is empty; we break before processing. Good.

Potential issue with early return n==k: If n==k, answer0. But what if k==n and n=0? constraints n>=1. Good.

Now, let's think about if a word i is in subtree of bad node v at depth d, but there is a longer prefix p of i at depth d' > d with cnt>=k after removal? If d' > d, then p is descendant of v. Since cnt[p] <= cnt[v] = k. For p to have count after removal >=k, need original cnt[p] >= k+1 if i has p, impossible because cnt[p]<=k. If p not prefix of i? But p is prefix of i by longer. So no. After removal cnt[p]-1 <=k-1 <k. So not allowed. If there is a descendant p not prefix of i? Not on path, doesn't matter. So indeed all deeper depths along that word are blocked. But what if there is another branch at deeper depth not prefix of i with cnt>=k? That branch is also in v subtree (since v unique good at depth d), and its count <=k. For it to be good at deeper depth, cnt>=k, so cnt=k. Then it is a bad node descendant. If word i is not in that branch, then at deeper depth, that branch is not prefix of i, so depth allowed? Wait if there is a descendant bad node w of v with cnt=k, and word i is in v subtree but not in w subtree, then at depth depth(w), w is a good node not prefix of i, so L allowed! Our earlier "if blocked at depth d, all deeper depths along i blocked" is false if there is another descendant branch with cnt=k. Let's examine.

Important! Suppose bad node v at depth d cnt=k. There are k words in v subtree. They may split into multiple branches at deeper depths. At depth d, all words in v subtree are blocked because removing any one reduces count of v to k-1. But at a deeper depth d' > d, there may be a prefix w in one branch with cnt=k (all k words share that longer prefix) or cnt<k. If cnt[w]=k, then w is a bad node at deeper depth. For a word i in v subtree but not in w subtree, at depth d', w is not prefix of i, so after removing i, count of w remains k, so depth d' is allowed! But can a word i in v subtree not have prefix w if cnt[w]=k and cnt[v]=k? If cnt[w]=k, that means all k words in v subtree also have prefix w, because cnt[w] cannot exceed cnt[v]=k. If cnt[w]=k, then every word in v subtree has prefix w. So no word in v subtree is outside w. Thus if a descendant node has cnt=k, it contains all words of v subtree. Therefore all words in v subtree are also in w subtree. So blocked at deeper too. If a descendant node has cnt<k, not good. Therefore no deeper allowed via another branch with cnt=k? Wait could there be two descendant nodes at same deeper depth, each with cnt < k but sum k, so no single prefix count>=k. Then depth not allowed. Thus if v cnt=k, any descendant node with cnt>=k must have cnt=k and contains all words in v subtree (because count cannot exceed k and to be >=k it equals k; since it's subset of v's k words, it must be the whole set). So all words in v subtree share that descendant prefix. Thus blocked along chain. Good.

But what about a descendant node w with cnt=k and word i in v subtree but not in w subtree? Impossible because w contains all v words. Good.

So chain property holds.

Now, let's think about if a word i in v subtree could be allowed at deeper depth due to a good node in a completely different part of trie outside v? Its ancestor at depth d would have cnt>=cnt[node]>=k, and would be outside v, contradicting uniqueness of v at depth d. So no. for idx, w in enumerate(words):

            children.append({})
            cnt.append(0)

            ends.append([])
            nodes_by_depth[d].append(nxt)
        node = children[node][ch]
        cnt[node] += 1
    ends[node].append(idx)

Now, let's think about if there is a time issue with DSU find in assign_range. Each position assigned once, but find called for each assignment plus for each range. Number of ranges: at most 2*bad_nodes + all_allowed depths. bad_nodes <= max_len <=1e4, all_allowed <=max_len. So <=3e4 range calls. Each find O(alpha). Good.

Now, let's think about if `assign_range` with a range that contains many already assigned positions but no unassigned could call find(l) and return >=r, O(alpha). Good.

Now, let's think about if `parent[p] = find(p+1)` and then `p = find(p)` could be optimized:
```
p = find(l)
while p < r:
    ans[order[p]] = val
    parent[p] = find(p + 1)
    p = parent[p]  # since parent[p] is root
```
But if parent[p] is root, yes. However, if p+1's find returns some root, parent[p] set to that root. So `p = parent[p]` works. But if parent[p] maybe not root due to path compression? It is set to root. Use `p = find(p)` for safety. Slight overhead but okay.

Now, let's think about if `find` iterative:
```
def find(x):
    while parent[x] != x:
        parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]
        x = parent[x]
    return x
```
This path halving. Good.

Now, let's think about if `parent` list of ints and `find` uses parent[parent[x]]; if parent[x] = n, parent[n]=n. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a prefix p with original cnt[p] == k and p is prefix of i, and count_good==1, but there is a longer prefix q not prefix of i with original cnt[q] == k? q descendant of p? Since p unique good at depth d, any good node at deeper depth must be descendant of p. If q descendant of p and cnt[q]=k, then q contains all p's k words, so q is also prefix of i? If i is in p subtree, and q contains all p words, then yes i has q. So q prefix of i. Thus blocked. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a prefix p with original cnt[p] == k and p is prefix of i, and count_good==1, but there is a longer prefix q not prefix of i with original cnt[q] == k? If q descendant of p and cnt[q]=k, q contains all p words, so q prefix of i. Contradiction. So no. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden issue with if a word i has length >= L and its prefix node at depth L is in G_L with cnt==k and unique, but there is another node at same
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestCommonPrefix(self, words: List[str], k: int) -> List[int]:
        n = len(words)
        if k > n or n == k:
            return [0] * n

        max_len = max(len(w) for w in words)

        children = [{}]
        cnt = [0]
        ends = [[]]
        nodes_by_depth = [[] for _ in range(max_len + 1)]

        for idx, w in enumerate(words):
            node = 0
            for d, ch in enumerate(w, 1):
                if ch not in children[node]:
                    nxt = len(children)
                    children[node][ch] = nxt
                    children.append({})
                    cnt.append(0)
                    ends.append([])
                    nodes_by_depth[d].append(nxt)
                node = children[node][ch]
                cnt[node] += 1
            ends[node].append(idx)

        num_nodes = len(children)
        tin = [0] * num_nodes
        tout = [0] * num_nodes
        order = []

        stack = [(0, False)]
        while stack:
            v, visited = stack.pop()
            if not visited:
                tin[v] = len(order)
                order.extend(ends[v])
                stack.append((v, True))
                for child in children[v].values():
                    stack.append((child, False))
            else:
                tout[v] = len(order)

        good_count = [0] * (max_len + 1)
        bad_node = [-1] * (max_len + 1)

        for d in range(1, max_len + 1):
            gc = 0
            bad = -1
            for v in nodes_by_depth[d]:
                if cnt[v] >= k:
                    gc += 1
                    if gc == 1:
                        bad = v
            good_count[d] = gc
            if gc == 1 and cnt[bad] == k:
                bad_node[d] = bad

        ans = [0] * n
        parent = list(range(n + 1))

        def find(x):
            while parent[x] != x:
                parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]
                x = parent[x]
            return x

        def assign_range(l, r, val):
            if l >= r:
                return
            p = find(l)
            while p < r:
                ans[order[p]] = val
                parent[p] = find(p + 1)
                p = find(p)

        cur_l, cur_r = 0, n

        for d in range(max_len, 0, -1):
            if cur_l >= cur_r:
                break

            bv = bad_node[d]

            if bv != -1:
                lv, rv = tin[bv], tout[bv]

                if lv <= cur_l and cur_r <= rv:
                    continue
                elif cur_l <= lv and rv <= cur_r:
                    assign_range(cur_l, lv, d)
                    assign_range(rv, cur_r, d)
                    cur_l, cur_r = lv, rv
                else:
                    assign_range(cur_l, cur_r, d)
                    cur_l = cur_r = 0
            else:
                if good_count[d] > 0:
                    assign_range(cur_l, cur_r, d)
                    cur_l = cur_r = 0

        return ans
```
